ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u1	I would like to welcome the Minister of the Interior and Kingdom Relations to the First Chamber. I give the floor to Mr Cliteur.
ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u2	Mr. Chairman. This is a special debate, because it is structured differently than most other debates in the Upper Chamber. In most other debates there is legislation ahead, which we as First Members of Parliament can comment on. Today there is no legislation before it, but a report. We are discussing this with each other, including the Minister who has not yet determined his position on the proposals contained in the report. All things considered, we haven't seen much of a cabinet response. Today, we are in the role of the advisory section of unfulfilled legislative proposals. We have to imagine what we hope will come up this way again. However, I do not mind, because it gives us the opportunity to outline some broad lines on the democratic rule of law, so I will at least take up our mission. Then I would like to begin by expressing my appreciation for the work done under the State Committee. It's a pleasure to read the report. It also contains a great deal of commendable material and I look forward to discussing it. In particular, I look forward to Mr Doornhof's maiden speech, which I would like to congratulate. But, of course, we also need to place some critical notes. Let me start by saying something about the title of the report. This report goes under no less than three titles. First of all, we have the main title: low thresholds, high dikes. I always find that a little confusing. You should then first go into the introduction to see exactly what the background is. Before you know it, you think: what was low and high again? Was it those thresholds or those dikes? We're getting a little more leverage with the first subtitle: report from the State Committee on Parliamentary System. That title too is not without dangers, because it is actually too little comprehensive. The parliamentary system is the system of democratic control introduced in the Netherlands in essence in 1848, under the king who looks at us every Tuesday through that report and who also wanted to be among us, reportedly, with the beginning of ministerial responsibility and thus the beginning of democracy. But today we are going to discuss far more than the parliamentary system. We are going to talk about the rule of law, and perhaps above all about it. For this reason, I think that the second subtitle is more solid: democracy and the rule of law in balance. I think that is a good title for two reasons. First of all, of course, because it is broad democracy and the rule of law, because that is what we are talking about today, but also because of the balance sheet. If the subtitle of the report of the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy contains the word 'balance sheet', then it may also be out of balance. There is a cautious suggestion that it could be out of balance at the moment. I find that very interesting. I think that suggestion is correct, as will be shown in the further course of my story. Democracy and the rule of law in balance. First of all, it is important that we ask ourselves what is actually a rule of law. You'll find a lot of different things about that. It's about to become a bit of a container concept. I can see that in the Minister of Justice's tweets. I saw that also in the speech of the throne, where a description of the rule of law comes along, which I thought: no, that's not him. The rule of law is the restriction of government power by law. It is very important that we keep that in mind. In doing so, I would like to describe democracy as 'the self-government of the citizens'. The rule of law has the ambition to be a state that limits its own power through law, through an organizational principle of separation of powers, the trias politicians, and by granting citizens certain individual rights that they can bring against that government. Then you already have a better description. There are then a number of important moments when you pass the history of the democratic rule of law. It begins in the distant past, in 1648, with the establishment of the national states. From that moment on, the world has been divided into about 200 nation states, but then it might have been a different number. We then know the monopoly organization of state power in the territory of the state, a very important moment in 1648. A century later we have in 1748 Montesquieu, who is revealed to the trias politician as an important principle and writes l'Esprit des lois. It restricts state power; this is a bit of the beginning of the rule of law. Then we go on for another 100 years and come to the beginning of ministerial responsibility in 1848. I just referred to it as "the king." The centre of gravity then shifts a little from the King to the ministers. That was a very important moment for democracy. Another century later, in 1948, we get the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. There is a global consensus on the importance of individual human rights as an instrument for expanding that state power. Another five years later, in 1953, we will work out this further under the Council of Europe in a European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, and we will be given a review mechanism. In fact, in the Netherlands, all Dutch courts are empowered to review laws against the binding provisions of the Treaties. The constitutional review shall then be rejected in Article 120 of the Constitution. So Article 120 Constitution rejects the national review option. Articles 93 and 94 of the Constitution then provide an opportunity to review laws against human rights from treaties. In 1953, that system of democratic rule of law was to some extent abolished. It's finished. We've worked on it for four centuries, and that's what it's all about. We need to balance the rule of law and democracy. So we now have a little over 50 years of experience in trying to balance that and keep it in balance. That is the system of a state (1648) who also wants to be a rule of law (1748), who also wants to be a democracy (1848) and who wants to respect human rights (1948) with an implementation mechanism in 1953. The question before us today, and I expect it to be in the years to come, is how pleased we are with this balance sheet. Do we think there is a balance and, if not, what is the problem? Then there are roughly three positions that you can distinguish. There will be a group that says: it is roughly in balance; it is actually fine; the existing system is functioning well; we must stop undermining far-reaching criticism of that system as soon as possible. That's the first position. The second position is: it is not quite in balance, and I am counting on the State Commission to do so, but with a number of adjustments we may still be able to get it in balance. The third position is: it's really not balanced at all, it's out of balance; we've been trying this for 50 years now, but there are still a number of problems. You won't be surprised that my party is in this position. I'm not going to tick off all those proposals from the state committee. That is going to happen this afternoon in the debate. I'm going to take one thing out of it that I think is a good manifestation of the thesis that the system is out of balance. I am going to say a few words about the relationship between the legislature and the judiciary. In particular, I would like to say something about this judicial review and whether the system is not out of balance and, if so, why. Mr. Chairman. It cannot have escaped the fact that the Supreme Court recently decided with the Urgendavonnis that CO emissions should be at least 25% lower by 2020 than in previous years. That has caused great turbulence, not only with my party, but with many others. To give an indication: I recently saw an article in the International Journal of Constitutional Law of a Spanish professor, who refers to this judgment as a landmark judgment. Well, that doesn't happen often, that a Spanish professor in an international magazine says something about a ruling from the Supreme Court. Well, obviously something's going on. The international community feels that very well. The Supreme Court itself, by the way, gives an English-language summary. So it is understood that something very fundamental has been done. The question is: how does this relate to the balance of the rule of law and democracy? We are then dealing with Article 21 of the Dutch Constitution, which says: "The concern of the government is aimed at the habitability of the country and the protection and improvement of the environment." That public care obligation is linked to Article 2 of the ECHR, right to life, and Article 8, right to respect for private life, family and family life. The crucial question, which you will find throughout the report of the State Committee on Parliament's system, is who, in the last sentence, should explain that concept of "life" from Article 2 of the ECHR. According to the positive law, this is, of course, the European Court of Human Rights in Strasbourg or the Dutch judge. That Dutch judge can also test against the ECHR. That's what the Supreme Court did in this Urgendavonnis. Is the legislator the first person to explain this or is that the judge? That's actually the big question. Since 1953, therefore, we have had a system in which this possibility of review exists for the judges. The question is: is that a happy situation? The critics of the Urgendavonnis then say that the concept of "life" is far too broad to be coloured by judges. Emeritus Professor of State Law Groningen Douwe Jan Elzinga says: If the judge is allowed to decide whether the climate measures are sufficient to protect life, then the judge can also decide whether you can still light fireworks. After all, that also has to do with life. What doesn't life have to do with? Abortion, euthanasia, road safety, war, peace, crime, why couldn't the judge even ban the car? After all, there are a lot of road accidents. Think of new legislation on completed life, which is now being discussed. The latter would be a very interesting test case for trust in the court: a situation in which our Supreme Court would review and declare inapplicable any legislation on completed life because of conflict with Article 2 of the ECHR. That would be very interesting. The moment we get there, I think there will be loud protests, especially from the left side of the political spectrum, about what we call "dive-rocation." I'm going to present a motion on it tonight. I hope that this House-wide support will be forthcoming; I expect that after you have heard what I am going to say about it. I'm going to say a single word about the word. Who introduced the word "dikastrocation" into the Dutch discussion? Who gave that word "dikastrocation" a very central place? Well, it occurs for the first time in the public committee meeting on 9 December 1974 in the Lower House. That debate was then attended by the former senator for the PvdA, Prof. Erik Jurgens. He was a member of the PvdA Group of the First Chamber from 1995 to 2007, and served for 4.382 days. A highly respected seniority and, moreover, a great state lawyer. I go back further, because prior to his membership of the First Chamber, Jurgens was a member of the House of Representatives. In 1974 he performed there on behalf of the PPR. During that debate, the Constitutional Review, the Constitutional Review, which is not possible in the Netherlands according to Article 120 of the Constitution, was also discussed. This review of laws against the Constitution continues to be discussed, again in the report of the State Commission. Jurgens is against it. He says that this constitutional review is actually contrary to democracy. If you would be for it, we get a dikastrocation in the Netherlands.He writes it with a c. He says on 9 December 1974: I am in favour of a democracy and I am not in favour of a dikastrocation or a government by judges. I quote him: 'If the judge decides what is contained in the Constitution and can therefore declare decisions of the national parliament unbinding, I think that is too big and too heavy a task for the judiciary, which was by no means laid down in the Netherlands for that purpose'. It is our former Member of Parliament, a state law professor Erik Jurgens, who in 1974, 39 years old, introduced the term "dikastrocation." He did so in the context of a discussion on the review of laws to the Constitution and thus on the balance between the rule of law and democracy. He is going to stand on the side of democracy and not on the side of the dikastocracy, as he himself puts it. The issue that Jurgens raises has actually been over the market since 1953, the completion of our system. The question is: what do we think after 50 years? The nice thing is that empirical material has recently been presented on this issue, the political assessment of laws by judges.

I wonder what direction he's thinking when he says the balance is going to go crazy. Where should the balance go? Should we restrict the judiciary? That is the big question we have to ask the Forum for Democracy Group at the moment.
ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u42	That is a very legitimate question. This question has been under discussion for a long time, including in the scientific literature on it. From 1803, there has been a discussion in the United States about whether a judge should exercise restraint, judicial restriction, or judicial activism. We at the Forum for Democracy are in favour of restraint. We believe that a judge should exercise restraint. Social change must not be achieved through the judiciary, but in principle according to the legislative power. That's an attitude we're asking of judges. But there's something else that goes with that. We need to be very careful when we go down that whole path of what needs to be done in the Constitution. We must guard against the proliferation of fundamental rights. We shouldn't make too many. We must be very careful whether a particular fundamental right is suitable for review. We must ensure that we do not give fundamental rights status that entails... Such a right to life, for example, is actually a life-threatening right, because it has everything to do with euthanasia, with war, with peace, with climate. Anything you can put in there. At the moment when you create such a right yourself as a legislator, you should not be surprised if at any time all the possibilities for legal regulation of something are in fact thrown out of your hands.

ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u46	Thank you, Mr. Cliteur. I now give the floor to Mr Kox.
ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u47	Mr. Chairman. Let me begin by thanking the State Commission for its proposals for the possible adaptation of our parliamentary system. My group has been in favour of the state committee from the outset and has been able to contribute to its creation, after the VVD announced in 2014 that it wanted to reflect on the role of the First Chamber in our parliamentary system for its muffled reasons. That opened up new perspectives for my party, which is convinced that we are protecting our democracy best by constantly strengthening it, including by doing great service to our parliamentary system. That is largely based, Mr Cliteur, on the Constitution, which was established in 1848. We see that Constitution there, on the portrait of King Willem II. It is a Constitution which the king would have preferred not to have seen, but in which he decided by means of a few constitutional steps, to prevent the revolution that had already taken hold of many parts of Europe here too. In order to preserve his crown, you can also see it on that beautiful painting, which the king himself had made as a special gift for this Chamber, William was willing to have the Constitution in force substantially revised. And so he appointed a state commission. By putting it under the leadership of the liberal foreman Thorbecke, the king knew that he would have to leave many feathers in favor of the parliament, which he had until then firmly under control. After all, the Second Chamber was elected by the fine fleur of the provinces, the members of Provincial States, and the First Chamber appointed the king himself, as his stronghold around the throne. Thorbecke wanted to get rid of such a powerful and autocratic kingship and on his way to a parliamentary system, in which not the King but Cabinet and Chamber would be the boss. The Chamber, because if it had been up to Thorbecke, in 1848 the First Chamber had been abolished and we had been given a single chamber system ever since. The fact that we exist today is thanks to that king and to the conservative majority in both Chambers at the time. By amendment, this Chamber was, against Thorbeckes' will, rewritten in the Constitution of 1848. That was the price of getting a majority of only one vote for Thorbecke's other proposals at first reading in this Chamber that year. It's just that we know how things can be. It was perhaps a good thing that these proposals were not presented to the Chamber by Thorbecke himself at the time, but by Minister Donker Curtius, who put together more diplomatically, I understood. Thorbecke would have said that the First Chamber was without ground and purpose and, if it came to it, would not be counted as useless delay or put aside if it dared to oppose the Second Chamber. It can all be read in the beautiful biography that Jan Drentje wrote about Thorbecke, if you ask where I get my knowledge from. Thorbecke was known to be in favour of taking power from the governorate and increasing the power of the law. And so he invented the constitutional monarchy, in which the King is inviolable and the ministers are responsible. These, together with the States-General, would henceforth make the laws of the country and be controlled by those States-General in their executive work. From now on, the Lower House was directly elected, although only by people with pennies, and the Upper Chamber kicked, by people with even more pennies. In 1848 it was still a little too early for popular sovereignty. The division of tasks between the two Chambers, in the words of Donker Curtius, would be that the House of Representatives was supposed to do the right thing, while the First Chamber was meant to prevent evil. It's a nice system. The Senate, as the auditor of the House of Representatives, should watch over his passionate sister, in the words of a nineteenth-century legal scholar. Later we liked to call ourselves chambre de réflexion. Today we say that we monitor the proposals presented to us by the government and the House of Representatives for necessity, efficiency, feasibility and enforcement, as well as the relationship with the Constitution and international treaties. That is correct, but at the same time this Chamber is also fully competent to control the government in all its executive tasks. Therefore, both chambers have similar rights, with the exception of the right of initiative and the right of amendment, which are reserved for the Lower House. Both chambers have an absolute right of veto, the right of investigation and budget law. Those aren't fake rights. The State Committee emphasizes the unprecedented strength of our Senate compared to abroad. The committee refers explicitly to the first parliamentary examination of this Chamber when it supports its proposal to introduce a law on decentralisation. If the Group of the Greens/European Free Alliance had strengthened its negative assessment of the tax plan for this year in December by voting against the rest of the opposition on the basis of the budget right granted to us, the First Chamber would have for the first time in 100 years effectively obliged the government to shape its budget for 2020 in a significantly different way. In that case, the Chamber would have actually re-directed the cabinet. But unfortunately GroenLinks didn't want to score this time, as a kind of reverse PSV, so to speak. Mr. Chairman. Anyway, after Thorbecke's constitutional revolution of 1848 the constitutional revolution of Cort van der Linden followed in 1917. His proposals led us to the idea of universal suffrage, proportional representation, the financial equivalence of public and special education and party democracy, including compartmentalisation. A proposal to abolish the First Chamber didn't make it to the second round. From now on, however, everyone could in principle be elected to this Chamber, regardless of income, title or CV. Thanks to this provision, we can now all sit here, from lord to dike, from professor to chess grandmaster, from specialist to publicist, and with a diehard socialist as a nestor. Poor Willem should have known all about it. Mr. Chairman. And now we all have to judge the proposals that Johan Remkes and his colleagues put to the government and parliament. Let me look at the main proposals and comment. I will always look at the Minister for the Interior and ask him to respond to my comments on the various aspects. After all, the government's support is indispensable for measuring now that the state committee has put its report on the table. Mr. Chairman. The State Committee proposes to maintain the two-chamber system, but to adapt the role of the First Chamber, in particular by introducing the return duty. That last proposal was already on the table in 2000, but we did not come to a conclusion because the debate was then suspended pending a position by the House of Representatives which has not yet been reached. My group still sees and sees the advantages of such a return right. In that structure, this Chamber, like now, can approve or reject the legislative proposals approved by the House of Representatives, but also bring them back to the House of Representatives in amended form. I believe that, on balance, this proposal will ensure better and faster legislation and a more effective role for this Chamber in the legislative process. Yes, therefore, is our opinion on the introduction of the right to return. Of course, we still have to talk about the modalities. Mr. Chairman. In my group's view, it would be better to move away from the two-chamber system and merge both chambers into the one State-General that we are actually already.
ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u48	It is interesting what Mr Kox says about the return right. Suppose the First Chamber sends a bill back to the Lower House in accordance with the proposals of the State Committee. The government and the House of Representatives then assess that, and according to the State Committee, that bill will not be returned to the Upper House. Suppose in the debate between the government and the Lower House it is said "we do not take into account what has been said in the Upper Chamber; we do not agree with that, so we leave it as it is," or that they give it a different twist. Is the position of the First Chamber strengthened or actually weakened if we do not get that proposal back? That is my question to Mr Kox.

Of course, we are constantly observing here: well, it could be, it could not be, this law, but those things should actually be different. We have such a complicated procedure with novelles and promises and all that. I think this would be a better method. We must try, but I do not see the danger of this Chamber getting worse. I was actually trying to explain what would be even better, namely that we would have a one-room system. In my group's view, that would be better. A one-room system is good to do. This proves the experience in the Scandinavian countries, which are certainly no less democratic than ours. At least one room is much better understood for the population. It lays the parliamentary burden directly where it belongs, namely the directly elected representative of the people. If it works well and is ultimately responsible for the legislation, an additional Chamber is no longer needed to watch over her eager sister. I can see why the state committee wouldn't have the courage to do that. Having the Rooms cut into their own meat does well and is not popular, but, as recalled, Thorbecke would be fine with it. A merged Chamber should probably have more than 150 members. The Netherlands is already a country with very few representatives of the people compared to the rest of Europe. That's something the State Commission is advocating anyway. So yes, if the state committee had made that proposal, we would certainly have said yes. But Johan Remkes is not a Thorbecke, and so he keeps his committee safe on the right to return, with which we as SP can, as said, also live well for the time being. We can do this all the more because the committee also makes some other proposals which could lead to greater coordination and cooperation between the two Chambers, such as making greater use of the existing structure of the unified meetings and possibly setting up joint committees. In particular, when it comes to an adequate review of the many far-reaching proposals of the European Commission, we believe that a common approach should be sought to ensure that Brussels legislation is effectively controlled by the Dutch Parliament. Almost all parties promised that after the impressive rejection of the European Constitution by the people in 2006. In the opinion of our group, both chambers cannot give this guarantee at the moment, despite all the efforts made, especially by this Chamber. Remarkably, the government has also put on the table a proposal on the First Chamber, which has just been rejected by the State Committee. The government wants to return to the system of election of the First Chamber in which half of the Senate was elected every three years. Let me be brief about this: we believe that nothing based nonsense will make the purpose and task of this Chamber even more unclear for our citizens. So don't. On both Chambers, the State Committee makes one major but extremely appropriate proposal, namely that proposals adopted by both Chambers may be subject to a corrective referendum. Given that my colleague Ronald van Raak is already busy on the other side of the House converting that plan into appropriate legislation, it should not be surprising that our group on this side of the Binnenhof welcomes that proposal from the committee as serious state-law renewal and substantial democratic improvement. Research indicates that a large proportion of the population is in favour of the corrective referendum through all political views. It is seen as a strengthening of our democracy. In particular, the people who are increasingly losing out because they feel that politics is not taking them seriously may get a sense of belonging to a referendum of this kind. So do, and as soon as possible, advise my group. We have some more doubts about the committee's proposal to introduce a constitutional court that makes it possible to review laws against the Constitution. The prohibition of review also dates from the Constitution of 1848. It certainly didn't come from Thorbecke, but probably from Dark Curtius. Anyway, it's still there. We have come no further than the Halsema legislative proposal adopted at first reading in this Chamber to enable a constitutional review of a number of fundamental rights by the judge. For this reason, it is remarkable that the State Commission is putting forward a far-reaching proposal: the establishment of a Constitutional Court. For the time being, our group would prefer to have the initiative legislative proposal Halsema approved by the Second and First Chambers at second reading. It will then be possible not only to invoke the European Convention on Human Rights of the Council of Europe in court but also to invoke the fundamental rights enshrined in the Dutch Constitution. The State Commission maintains the system of proportional representation; old ideas about the re-establishment of a district system have apparently been finally put to death. We're fine with that. However, the Committee wishes to better reflect the personal and regional component of the electoral system for the Lower House. Whether or not that brings politics closer to people is the question for us. Politics is human work, but it is more than individual voters choosing individual candidates. Our parliamentary democracy has been closely linked to political parties since 1917. With all the reservations about how political parties are currently operating, we still believe that a political party gives people the opportunity to take sides in political directions related to political ideologies. Democracy is more than and more difficult than choice between individuals. Whoever chooses will guide the future; this will preferably have to be done on the basis of strong ideas and proposals. The Committee recognizes this and makes proposals which should make it possible for people to explicitly choose a party as such and only if they so wish for an individual of a party. Our preliminary assessment is that we need to think again about this. This is also true of the committee's conclusion that there should not be an obligation to vote, arguing that voting is a right but not a duty. My group has not yet reached that conclusion. Paying taxes is a duty, because financing political choices is a necessary condition for a functioning democracy. Participation in education is a right, but it is also a duty, because training young people is a prerequisite for a solid foundation under society and democratic law. You adhere to laws and rules that are made by politics is a duty because they respect the rights of others. Then why shouldn't it be a civic duty to say who should represent you in decisions about laws and rules? In our view, there is a close link between law and duty, especially when it comes to the essence of democratic society. I would like to have a more detailed debate on this position. As I said, we are also thinking about this. When it comes to raising the electoral threshold and raising demands on political newcomers, our group is clear: do not start. We must keep our political system open to newcomers, no matter how strange and disturbing their ideas may be. We find it deeply regrettable that some people in this Chamber are calling for the categorization of population groups, contrary to Article 1 of the Constitution. We will continue to fight this here in this Chamber at all times, with arguments, with facts, in the hope that the voters will eventually see through and deal with political opportunism. However, restricting the parties' rights through higher electoral thresholds, guarantees or necessary statements of support or, even more far-reachingly, banning them through a law on political parties, we are not satisfied. Citizens should in principle be free to organise themselves in a political party on the basis of their political views, to whatever side they may move. In our opinion, the existing legislation already allows the courts to prohibit a party if it has the characteristics of a criminal organisation. You understand that our general line is here: extreme restraint. However, we agree with the committee that the formation of political groups through division after elections should be avoided as much as possible. After all, electoral deception and robbery should not be rewarded. A final far-reaching proposal from the committee to increase the involvement and influence of citizens is the introduction of an elected reformer. That is certainly a possibility that has already been mentioned in the past. The question is whether that is the best option. Our group is more concerned with encouraging political blocking before the elections with consequences for post-election government formation. That is not a unique proposal. In a country like Denmark, it already works something like this. The block with the largest electoral supporters may take the initiative of creating a new government in such a structure. Political blocing in advance on the basis of an agreed public election manifesto has been proposed several times by my party in the past. That it has not yet come to pass is history; we now write the future. For the time being, we would prefer this variant to the chosen formatter, because then it is not the person, but the political content that is dominant and best understood by the citizens if they make their choice. In summary. Our group is very pleased with what the state committee has put on the table. And since the State Committee has been set up on the actual initiative of this Chamber, this Chamber must also pay due attention to it. We owe it to the Remkes State Commission to do the best with its solid advice, and please speed it up. It doesn't have to be Echternacher Procession. As a reminder, this king, Willem II, established the Thorbecke-State Commission on 17 March 1848. Less than eight months later, on 3 November of that year, the radically revised Constitution was already proclaimed and a constitutional revolution was a fact. Those were the days. Eight months! As I said, the speed at that time was closely linked to the rapid social developments outside the Chambers, in their own country and abroad. It was evident at that time that those who did not adapt would be adapted from the outside. That was also the case in 1917. The fact that it came to an extra-parliamentary revolution in the Netherlands in 1848 or in 1917 was due to the fact that government and parliament were finally able to understand and act on the signs of time. Mr. Chairman. In 2020, as far as I can see, there is no threat of an extra-parliamentary revolution. However, there is widespread discontent about the actual functioning of democracy and our parliamentary system, as the State Committee also reports. Many people, especially those with lower education and lower incomes, often feel unheard or insufficiently heard, although they would like to. That is why they are generally in favour of democracy, but are far less positive about us politicians and the functioning of the parliamentary system. Which means we're living in a risky time now, too. Those who look around hear the lure of authoritarian politicians and see the advance of authoritarian systems. If we are to defend our democracy, we must renew and improve our democracy. The state committee's report can help us with that. As has already been said, my group is very pleased with it and is keen to work towards the necessary maintenance of our democracy. In our humble opinion, that makes us all better. Thank you.

ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u53	Mr. Chairman. I too would like to begin on behalf of my group by thanking the Remkes Committee, which has produced an important and thorough report. That report and, above all, the cabinet response are at the heart of the debate today. We started in 2014. In 2016 this became even more concrete with the Duthler motion, in which the first consideration was called the involvement of the Dutch citizen in policy and politics. We are discussing today the analysis of the State Commission and, in particular, the first cabinet response to it. The chairman of the State Committee expressed his disappointment at the slow reaction of the government in the discussions with the Committee on Home Affairs. Indeed, the second cabinet response is still missing, so that we only have a limited part of the story on the table. But in addition to slowness, there is sometimes inappropriate speed. But we would like to see a little more analysis, because this composition and also the feeling of not being represented do not mean that the views and interests of these groups of citizens are not sufficiently represented. It's not the same thing. Is there a deficit in the representation of content, or is there a deficit in the extent to which citizens feel involved? Both problems are very serious to take, but they do require a completely different solution. For example, a stronger regional accent in national elections may be useful when it comes to involvement, but in Dutch relations it is unlikely to have an effect on the substantive representation of positions. We must also ask whether the Netherlands is not too small to expect much of this, unlike France or the United States, for example. The government writes that greater regional commitment contributes to improved substantive representation. We would like an explanation of that. Does "contentual" mean political representation or subjectively polite representation? Same goes for age. Young people and older people are less present among the people. The same is certainly true for Dutch people with a migration background. In these groups there are perhaps even more differences in terms of content and political emphasis to be expected than in terms of region. But the government does not choose to come to that point with adjustments. Why is only regional union really important to the cabinet? Can the government share its reflections on this point? Why is this form currently being chosen? And what are the powers of such a youth parliament? Is it more than a democratic pacifier? And how is representativeness guaranteed? We're not necessarily against it, but the question is what and how and why. The top seven of the proposals of the State Committee also includes the referendum. The committee is very clear about the importance of a specific form, namely a binding corrective referendum which clearly excludes certain issues and makes firm demands on turnout. The government would like to think further about this, also in relation to the motion tabled by our group member, Mr Lintmeijer, which called for a vision of new forms of direct democracy. We are still waiting for that vision. It is clear to my group that there is a need for forms of direct democracy, but also that it is essential that it is not a sweetener and that it must therefore be very clear what happens to the outcome of civil fora, deliberations and/or referenda. It seems that the report sees these forms of civic participation more as involvement than as substantive influence or direct democracy. This means that the risk of disappointment among citizens is just as great as in the consultative referendum. If we do it, we have to do it right. The question is therefore also how to facilitate democratic participation in the social domain and in what forms we can bring it back into politics. I would ask the Minister if he could give us a hint of the options he sees in order to really enhance direct democracy. I would also like to make a comment on all of this. The extent to which citizens and voters feel involved and represented may also be linked to the way in which we act as representatives of the people. The spread of lying racist tweets does contribute to polarisation in society, but does not contribute to confidence in democracy and the rule of law. To insist that such a tweet is correct or to play half-hearted when the lie comes true doesn't. It also says something about us as representatives of the people how we act and what we contribute to democracy and the rule of law. Know, by the way, that voters sometimes feel deceived by our political compromises, not because of the compromise itself, which is part of life, but because for the sake of electoral gain, we are calling harder for the bühne, while parties sometimes support laws that are at odds with them. In fact, we often simplify the political struggle to merely power and interests, and do not openly engage in the conflict of ideas that should be discussed. Maybe we can charge ourselves with all that. After all, the big question to which voters deserve an answer is what our great ideas are for this society and for this world. It should be a little bit more about that. The political struggle of nails on low tide is nice for the political daily rates and especially electoral daily rates, but it may also be one of the deeper reasons that people lose confidence in the parliament. You can't solve that with a structural change. The second is the resilience of the democratic rule of law. We welcome the fact that this has been the subject of more discussion in recent years, as this is a sign of the importance of the rule of law. We see in the cabinet response many elements that could contribute to resilience, including improved legislation for political parties and strengthening citizenship education. It is not so good that this debate on the rule of law is being used as it was this afternoon in order to discredit important institutional guarantees of the rule of law, with the argument that the parliament should have the last word. That was a bit of the discussion we had with Mr Cliteur about whether democracy should be cannibalised under the rule of law or whether it is happening the other way around. I think that is an important question: where is the balance? Herman Tjeenk Willink also covered this week in an essay in De Groene. He warned that politicians who made the justice system structuralally suspicious would also make the democratic rule of law vulnerable. That is why we have difficulty, I say to the Minister, in saying that the government writes that constitutional review leads to a distortion of state power, an increase in the influence of the judge on the political process and politicisation of the judiciary. We also see the guarantees of the rule of law in the independent judge, who is not leading politics and who can also call the government to account if she does not fulfil her own legal duties. And even though a judge doesn't always say what I want, I'm happy with that independent judge. It is part of the checks-and-balances that prevent a majority of 51% from pushing unfair and unlawful decisions. That is why my question to the government is: how is a judge assessing the Constitution a distortion between state powers or a form of politicisation of the judiciary? Then why is this the case and is it not the case when we check against international treaties? I would like to give a more detailed explanation of this rather subjective and suggestive comment by the government. We do share the analysis that strengthening the rule of law, especially in our polarised climate, is necessary. The legal protection of citizens is at stake. In our view, this requires a form of constitutional review. Of course, as representatives of the people, we should do so ourselves, especially in this Chamber. Unfortunately, however, there are too many examples where the constitutionality of certain measures is at least controversial and is mentioned here in the debate, but is not taken seriously by majority. Politicians may tend to consider the desirability of a measure more important than constitutionality. Especially when it comes to citizens' fundamental rights, Parliament's scrutiny is therefore not sufficient. That's how honest I think we should be. ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u93	Mr. Chairman. Let's see how this is done in Germany. In Germany, government policy is governed by a number of principles. First of all, the Ressortprinzip, each minister has his own responsibility and the Kollegialprinzip, one works Collegially together. But German state law also knows the important Richlinienkompetenz or the Kanzlerprinzip, according to Article 65 of the Grundgesetz, whereby the chancellor can give instructions to individual ministers regarding the coalition's overarching policy. The chancellor may declare certain matters chiefsache and intervene. That's how you take control. That's how we can do it. What is also sometimes seen in Germany, but much more often in Britain, is a so-called cabinet reshuffle: well-functioning ministers are promoted by the Prime Minister to more important posts and poorly-functioning ministers are dismissed and replaced by better rulers. We believe that this possibility should also be introduced in the Netherlands. The composition of the ministerial team is now far too static. There should be more room for mid-term goodbye to non-performing ministers.

We are therefore also tabling a motion to support the idea of abolishing the First Chamber as a First Chamber, so that all the emergency measures proposed by the Cabinet can be avoided. Mr. Chairman. Then there are the measures that the cabinet will not simply take over, or extend for itself until after the Lower House elections, towards a subsequent cabinet. It is particularly striking not to take up directly the proposal of the State Committee on the binding corrective referendum. D66 Minister Ollongren took the citizens' consultative corrective referendum with this cabinet. And now it turns out that in the end, under this cabinet, nothing will come back. We were presented with'something better', but when it comes down to it, the citizen has only been deprived of democratic participation. This sham must be punished quickly with the red pencil. Another proposal that the cabinet does not take up directly is the Constitutional Court, and that is a good thing, because it would give even more influence to activist justice. Nevertheless, the cabinet is considering other variants, including variants in which the judge does not issue a binding judgment, but gives an opinion to the legislator. That makes the judge even more political. Rather leave the Constitutional Court completely out of it. Furthermore, the cabinet intends to consider lowering the electoral age from 18 to 16 years. Can the Minister indicate whether young people aged 16, who are given adult responsibilities with the right to vote, will henceforth no longer be subject to juvenile criminal law but to adult criminal law, as will all other legal obligations and responsibilities associated with adulthood? Finally. The State Commission strongly calls on the legislator to take seriously the nature and extent of the social divide and the dangers that threaten our democratic rule of law both inside and outside the political system. In particular, let us mention these dangers, such as ignoring referendum results, abolishing referendums, excluding parties despite great electoral support, shady agreements, prosecuting a politician for his views, political appointments within an administrative regent class and increasingly transferring powers to unelected bureaucrats in Brussels. What should be taken up quickly and effectively is the return of our power from Brussels, a nexit, and the giving of real influence to our citizens through referenda. The main causes of social division are Islamization, mass immigration and the ruler elite. This country does not need such cowardly fake people's representatives, but real people's representatives, who keep their word and thus their election promises. That can't be solved by a state committee.

We didn't know that yet, but Akkerman and her fellow researchers say that based on really extensive research. This also applies to people who are already struggling with major differences of power in society. They are therefore also more susceptible to populism, which divides the world into a we and a she. This is also apparent from Akkerman's research. First, the low thresholds. You get all gloomy when you hear all this. There is indeed enough to improve, but I myself always hold on to the plus points. It is important that people feel that they no longer have control over their own lives. Then they will also drop out of the democratic system. Perhaps we should look for a solution not in the democratic system, but rather at the points where we are failing. I liked the fact that the committee described the participatory paradox, because in trying to involve citizens better, you reach the citizens who are already active. I found the idea of the youth parliament that the cabinet is going through, such an example. Then maybe you should think of other ways to get these people involved. Back to the report. I would like to take out two measures within this pillar that lie within the democratic system. The first is the reconnaissance that the cabinet is doing, such an A proposal as Mrs Vos indicated, the exploration to increase the possibilities for individual candidates to choose more on their own. The regional component also pays attention to this. My group is in principle open to this type of proposal, but I would like to specify a limit, because the function of MP is also a craftsman. Recognizability is one of the qualities, but not the only one. Such a preferred voice should not weigh too heavily. People not only vote for candidates, but also for a political party. Political parties tend to pay attention to skills and regional distribution in their recruitment lists. Then it is indeed a bit of a paradox that most political parties are decreasing in size at the moment, while they have a very important role to play in maintaining the democratic system. The Forum for Democracy rightly pointed out in the committee that since then it has also been extensive in the publicity that they are the exception. We will continue to monitor their development closely in the coming years. Before I come to a further proposal within this pillar, I would first like to explore the current political electoral image on behalf of my group. The polarization of the 1970s seems to be back. The volatility of the electorate has increased. Political fragmentation is also increasing. In a subsequent cabinet formation, perhaps five parties are needed for a majority. Several municipalities already have to deal with this. That means a lot of compromises. My group chairman has already mentioned in the General Political Affairs Committee the consequences for the feasibility of legislation if too many compromises have been built on each other. In addition to majority voting, democracy also means that there is attention and respect for minority positions. Individual freedoms and rights should be protected. Parliament should assess individual proposals not only on their merits, but also on the basis of wishes in other policy areas and on the implications for future generations. The latter is certainly important at this time, when we are faced with a number of long-term issues that will affect future generations in particular. I refer to climate change and demographic developments, which in turn have consequences for affordability and care, old age provision and the functioning of the labour market and the housing market. The renewal of our instruments in the parliamentary system must, above all, be mirrored against this reality, according to my group. In this context, our group is very reluctant to make proposals in which digital choices are 'digital' in the spirit of yes or no which, in our view, strengthen polarisation. The Committee stresses that the strengthening of the democratic rule of law requires not only democratic and constitutional institutions, but also a democratic and constitutional culture of the main actors within it. ParlaMint-NL_2020-02-04-eerstekamer-6.u226	Mr. Chairman. Firstly, I wish Mr Doornhof every success in his maiden speech, which he will be holding after me. Mr. Chairman. Today, 4 February, we said goodbye to former colleague Mrs Van Huffelen, because of her appointment as Secretary of State for Finance. I congratulated her this morning and wished her wisdom and strength. You will understand that I cannot fail to note that with this appointment she is the embodiment of the motion I tabled on 10 December last year to appoint a second Secretary of State for Finance. The motion was held by me on 16 December, one day later the then Secretary of State for Finance, Mr Snel, announced his resignation in the House of Representatives. It can happen quickly, Mr President, and now we have two. Mr. Chairman. A lot has been said. I will confine myself to those subjects that directly concern the Upper House itself. I just gave a small introduction to interrupting earlier. This opportunity does not occur often, if not rarely. The final report of the Parliamentary Committee offers an excellent opportunity. I agree with many of my colleagues who have expressed their appreciation for the report. The 50PLUS Group thinks that the opinion is complete, sound and I would like to say this here too: excellent in terms of language. It evokes the interest in a manner of speaking more and more while you are reading. That's what I like about this piece. The Committee describes three tasks for the First Chamber. Providing a counterweight to the actual monism in the House of Representatives. For this it is necessary that senators are not bound by the coalition agreement. Secondly, a better guarantee of the rule of law, the enforcement and the feasibility of legislative proposals, especially when they have not received sufficient attention in the previous stage of the legislative process. Thirdly, to strengthen the resilience of the democratic rule of law. I now come to the committee's speech on this matter. The political composition of the Chambers is increasingly different from each other and the committee recognizes the problematic side of it, but these factors do not outweigh the value of the First Chamber as a democratic correction mechanism at some distance from the government and the coalition agreement. The Committee states that the added value of the First Chamber is not sufficiently reflected as part of the entire system of checks-and-balances, power and counter-power. In order to make the added value more effective, the Committee considers that some distance from the government and the House of Representatives is desirable. Well, according to the committee, the First Chamber is a part-time parliament and is not involved in the cabinet formation. Senators of coalition parties should not be bound or bound to the coalition agreement. The role of the First Chamber as a correction mechanism is rarely reflected in the results of the votes. Each year, on average, one to two legislative proposals are rejected, out of the 200 to 300 proposals. The number of forced novelles is also small, especially in comparison with the number of amendments in the House of Representatives, and I speak from some experience when I say that. There are also more and more initiatives recently. Mr. Chairman. According to the Committee, the added value of the First Chamber could be better achieved if it had a less blunt instrument by giving it a return right. The Committee points out a previous report by the Temporary Committee of the First Chamber: the First Chamber is also a political body in which legislative proposals are determined in the light of party programmes and principles. Despite the focus on legislative quality, the review of legislative proposals is ultimately of a political nature. Between 2010 and 2017, the Committee notes, the Cabinets Rutte I and Rutte II ruled without a solid coalition majority in the First Chamber. This led to negotiations between cabinet and coalition and opposition groups in the Lower House, expecting a broadening of political support in the Lower House to lead to the adoption of a bill by the Upper Chamber, and otherwise not. The Committee points out that the First Chamber itself has only a limited role in this process. Old senator De Grave could formulate that nicely: it is the seats in the First Chamber, not the ideas used in the political negotiation process in the Second Chamber. The Committee states that this is an unintended effect of the two-chamber system. In fact, the composition of the Upper House thus leads to a higher majority for legislative proposals in the Lower House. The Committee also notes that the legislative production of Rutte I and Rutte II has in no way come to a standstill. If a subsequent cabinet reacts less agilely to "running opposition via the Senate," decision-making could come to a standstill, which is particularly important in exceptional circumstances in the country. The Committee also points out that the First Chamber itself played a greater role under Rutte I and II: more novelles, more rejected proposals, and the Cabinet also withdrew more proposals due to the lack of sufficient support in the First Chamber. The First Chamber thus acts a little more as a correction mechanism vis-à-vis government and coalition groups in the Lower Chamber, if the government does not have a fixed majority in the Upper Chamber, the Committee says. The Committee sees this as a positive side effect of increased electoral volatility. The Committee advocates that groups in the Lower House do not give the impression that they determine the voting behaviour of their party members in the Upper House. Conversely, senators should not give the impression that their attitude is a direct derivative of the voting behaviour of their party members in the other Chamber. If this impression arises, the Committee says, this undermines the added value and the role of the First Chamber in the state system. The Committee concludes that the First Chamber is a counterweight to the strong monistic relations, the increasingly narrower majorities and the more detailed government agreements that have been taken over by the House of Representatives over the past decades. I speak from some experience when I see here that under the present cabinet in the Lower House the engine block of 76 seats actually smothers every debate, but at least every question about the result in advance. Well, at least we still have a First Chamber. The Committee points out that more routes have been developed by the First Chamber to exert more influence besides the blunt weapon of rejection. The novelle, a new bill which amends the original bill, has been criticised from a state-owned point of view as a disguised right of amendment, but formally, according to the committee, it is a power of the government and not of the First Chamber. Nor has the Council of State declared the novelle instrument contrary to the Constitution, provided that it is used in a sparing manner. Mr. Chairman. The cabinet responds to the committee's opinion as follows. The potential added value of the First Chamber for the system as a whole is not sufficiently clear. The committee is putting forward the proposal for a return right. The committee also makes a second proposal to improve the coherence between the work of the two chambers: an adaptation of the procedure for revising the Constitution, the so-called second reading at a united meeting. And now he comes: the cabinet, they say, adds a third proposal, namely the proposal to elect the First Chamber in a different way, namely in the way before 1983. I go into more detail about the cabinet's proposal for a different way of electing the First Chamber, and I start with what the committee says about that. The Committee does not recommend changing the way in which the Upper Chamber is elected. The change in the term of office from six to four years was in fact originally intended. It has already been recalled here in conjunction with the transition to a direct election of the First Chamber. There was no majority before that election. The committee notes that there was no in-depth debate at the time on the length of the session. One of the arguments put forward was that the length of the term of office and the election in two halves meant that the composition would be a little further away from the term of office and then the voter's current preferences, which was considered undesirable. Moreover, there was no need for the greater stability of the First Chamber which could result from a term of six years. The Committee notes that it has not happened once since 1983 that a coalition lost its majority during the journey. I would point out to Mr Remkes that in December, too, Mr Remkes said to the committee that it is a coincidence that shortly after this opinion it was the first time that the cabinet lost the majority in this Chamber during the journey. The committee notes that the differences between the two systems are small. The First Chamber has been more active and self-conscious since the sixties, but this was not caused by the change in 1983. Well, it is, and that's where every four years there's speculation that the coalition loses the majority in the First Chamber. The delayed pass-through of voters' preferences in the First Chamber was one of the reasons for the state committees Van Schaik and Cals to move away from the election in two parts. The Committee considers that the delayed effect will undermine the right of the First Chamber to exist. Finally, the Committee notes that another side effect of a return to the system prior to 1983 is that it becomes more difficult for small parties to enter the Upper Chamber. I quote: there are only 37 or 38 seats to be allocated at each election, instead of 75, which actually leads to a doubling of the electoral threshold. The reaction of the cabinet to the phased election of the First Chamber is the following. I quote: 'The State Committee stresses the value of the First Chamber as a democratic correction mechanism at some distance from the government and the coalition agreement. The cabinet believes that it would be better to return to the system before 1983 in this light. The longer term of the senators, their indirect election and the delayed passage of change in the political power relations in the First Chamber fit better with the role and position of the First Chamber as a chambre de réflexion. It can therefore no longer be the case that the Upper House as a whole has a more recent political mandate than the Lower House." It's been noted. The final judgment of the cabinet is: with the method of election of the First Chamber thus to be modified, a better division of roles between the two Chambers is contributed. To this end, this reform is in line with the government's intention to introduce the return right and the change in the Constitutional revision procedure requested by the Cabinet. These three proposals therefore form a coherent whole. Those who still know that the Hoekstra motion has been adopted by a large majority here will understand why I am saying this so slowly and clearly. My group believes that the committee is putting forward convincing arguments not to proceed to the election of the First Chamber in two stages. It will be clear that, for this reason, my group is not in favour of the gradual election of the Supreme Court because of too great an impact on small parties. When I read and tell you this, I note that the cabinet's proposal is a transparent, fragile political game in the power struggle between government and control, between government and parliaments. I'd say, "First Chamber, keep an eye on your case." Finally, the return right. I shall come back to that because it also requires an amendment to the Constitution. The committee provides a thorough basis. It recommends that the First Chamber be given the power to amend a bill which has been accepted by the House of Representatives. If the First Chamber does so, then he must return the proposal to the House of Representatives. He then decides whether to accept the amendment of the First Chamber or to reverse it completely. The House of Representatives then decides whether the proposal as a whole is adopted or not. The First Chamber may therefore choose to adopt, reject or return the proposal. If the First Chamber chooses to return, the last word is and will remain for the House of Representatives, unless there are further rules, but I will not go into that. If the First Chamber does not use the right to return, he will retain the last word. The power of the Supreme Court to reject a proposal is thus maintained. In each proposal, he may decide separately whether he wishes to exchange his right of rejection for a one-off return right. The First Chamber will then be given the opportunity to give a concrete indication of how the proposal can be improved. Finally, I would like to say a few words about the committee's recommendation that, if necessary, a constitutional review should be carried out ex post on fundamental rights by means of a new constitutional court. We ask the Minister to make a note of the advantages and disadvantages of that recommendation, in which other variants can also be involved by the cabinet, as the cabinet itself suggests. I would like to endorse Mr Verkerk's question as to whether the Minister will undertake to return to it by weighing up the pros and cons and involving other variants. I look forward to the Minister's reply with interest.

That's easy to say, but it's not easy to do. So you have to make something that is sustainable on the one hand and at the same time resistant to the changing society. Many have said it: that change is moving faster and faster. The world is actually getting smaller and smaller. All this has implications for the way in which we relate to each other in a parliamentary system. In its investigation, the State Committee has shown that the functioning of the parliamentary system must be placed within the broader framework of the democratic rule of law. It also points to a number of current developments that cause concern. That is why, after 100 years, it is time for adjustments to our democracy and our rule of law, according to the State Commission. The first theme in the Remkes Committee's report was representativeness, or lack thereof. That's what we see from all kinds of phenomena. That is what we see when we talk about young people or certain groups of people who feel totally unrepresented. We can see from the way the young people have manifested themselves around the climate marches. At the same time, we also know that the system means that majorities in the population do not automatically translate into majorities in parliament. That's a fact. In fact, people who voted for a particular party do not always agree with the views of the party after it has been elected. If there is a government agreement that makes agreements that are very good for the country's governance, but which means that parties are on a smaller playing field than they might want, then it will be even more difficult to establish that connection. Then the question is, what are you going to do about it? What are you gonna do to deal with that problem? We have announced the first tranche of measures. I shall now turn to a number of questions raised by Mr Vos, Mr Schalk and Mr Ganzevoort. The first is, of course, to see what you can do about representativeness, about the electoral system, where many have the feeling: who am I now as an individual voter to decide who is elected on my behalf? Can't you do more about that representativeness? Of course, different types of electoral system have been designed or imaginable, including internationally; you can make a whole comparison. In the end, however, the cabinet believes that the preferred vote should play an important role, in order to give the electorate more influence. That still does not mean that Mr Kox is going to say everything that anyone who votes for him may expect of him, but it will create a link between the electorate and the elected. Internationally it also turns out that this just works. The participation of young people is also very important. I will come to that in a moment when we are talking about the youth parliament. The new generation is going to play a very important role, because they are ultimately the heirs of those who are sitting here now and who are generally of a slightly older age. They will have to maintain and further shape democracy and the rule of law. Citizenship and the awareness of what country you live in, where you come from, what is your common history and what is a rule of law, are principles that cannot be exposed enough. That is precisely the reason why a bill on this matter has been submitted to the Lower House by the colleague for Education. We are also going to promote the whole phenomenon of internet consultation. Digital society has threats in it, as Mr Verkerk has just pointed out, but it also has a lot of opportunities in it. One of the opportunities that you also see on social media is, of course, that the distance between the voter and the elected can be much smaller, and also the distance between the voter and the board. We are working on programmes to communicate more clearly and to speak more easily with our citizens. We do not want to make the citizen feel like a number stuck in a machine, in the bureaucracy that we unfortunately simply need, but we want to be clear: what do we ask of citizens and what is our message? That's in very simple things sometimes, but not simple things. That's a small difference about how you speak and how you communicate. At the moment, this programme is also being closely followed by local authorities. We are working on the Democracy in Action programme, in which we want to use instruments around e-democracy that allow voters to participate. I also notice from the cabinet that in the whole mindset, with all kinds of things we do, there is more and more the idea: how can you use that digital element to involve citizens? The second theme of the Remkes Committee's report concerns the vulnerability of the democratic rule of law. I think that people who may have had it with The Hague, as it is said so easily, can lose faith not only in the Hague politics, but also in the whole system, in the phenomenon of democracy. That's really dangerous. Then we go on a path where what we have built is all taken for granted, and in which what is dear to us is no longer cherished. We need to work on that. We cannot be naive about foreign interference and digital vulnerabilities either. We will have to resist this, as we have had to do in previous times against other threats. What did the cabinet ultimately do with a number of proposals in this area? There is, among other things, the Political Parties Act, which is currently in preparation. This leads us to look at a number of points on how we can organise things better and make things better in time. How can we translate the threats that exist now, but not 20 years ago, into good legislation? So that is not interference in internal party matters, but accountability in the context of transparency in an open democracy and the appointment of a number of rules of the game. The Political Parties Act is currently in preparation. That bill will be put into action this year. We have also drawn up a strategy against disinformation. This was sent to the House of Representatives in October 2019. There will also be a follow-up to this when it comes to the desired regulation. That will have to be done in line with the developments that are taking place in Europe, because we are not on an island. We are also dealing with all kinds of movements between countries. The procedure for the appointment of members of the Supreme Court is also amended. That bill was brought into consultation on 24 December last. Another proposal involves a process of internet consultation. I was just talking about that. There is also a strong call for attention to be paid to aspects of a constitutional nature. That's actually some kind of constitutional review ex ante. This will be further developed this year. These are all examples that in themselves may not be very shocking or cause very big changes, but in combination they take care of that necessary maintenance. Mr Schalk wondered whether I could indicate which parts of the top seven of the state committee fit in with what the government intends to do. Many of these measures will be discussed shortly, but I can go over them briefly. First of all, the adjustment of the electoral system. That's what we're doing, and I'll be back to talk about it in a minute. As far as the binding corrective referendum is concerned, there is currently an initiative proposal. I thought Mr Meijer said just now that this is a lack of direction. Actually, I don't see it that way. I actually see it as the power of parliament when the Lower House uses the right of initiative and starts working on it itself. That is not to say how the cabinet will ultimately appreciate it, because we are not there yet, but the fact that the proposal is there is worth a compliment, especially if you know how much work it is to make an initiative proposal. The cabinet has said not to take over the recommendation on the chosen formatter, but we do want to study the cabinet formation together with the House of Representatives. The constitutional review by a court is currently subject to consultation in the bosom of the cabinet and within the coalition to see which variant could be workable or not. Transparency about the use of digital instruments by political parties is taken into account in the new Political Parties Act. As regards improving democratic knowledge and skills in education and beyond, there is a bill in the House of Representatives, as I said just now. The right of return, which many of you have already considered and which many of you have philosophized in terms of the possibilities that there would be, is currently also the subject of consultation on the second package and the question is how exactly we intend to shape it. I consider this debate to be an enrichment of the various positions. The palette of different positions is wide in this Chamber and an additional contribution to the preparation that we are about to take.

I therefore endorse Mr Verkerk's view that it is also about that democratic, constitutional culture and not just about how you regulate it. Let us bear that in mind when new debates are held on this issue. Then the position of the First Chamber. I shall then come back to speak on the Schalk motion. There is, of course, a natural division of roles between the two chambers, which has grown organically over the course of the two centuries. It looks at policy-making and the legislative process, including the whole process of ground law. It's not entirely unwritten. The Constitution does indeed provide points of reference. For example, the First Chamber does not have the right of initiative or the right of amendment. In this context, Mr Kox has often been referred to me as Minister Donker Curtius. He opposed Thorbecke's idea of abolishing the First Chamber, because it would have no added value. He replied already in 1848: "that in general the utility of the First Chamber, however composed, lies more in preventing evil than in founding good." In practice today, this role is mainly reflected in the great attention paid to the legality, feasibility and enforcement of legislative proposals, but at the same time, of course, the First Chamber is also simply a political body. That's what came up tonight. It's just a political organ. When people say "it's too political," you might say that you may not be doing other things right, but it's just wrong to deny that there's political practice here. That is not the reality. It is true that the reflective role of the Chamber stands in a kind of balance relationship to political profiling. So you could say that one doesn't always relate to the other. That explains the reluctance that we often see in the Chamber's attitude, even this evening, I must say. If you don't spend so much time in the Upper House, then it's special to really encounter a different way of debating than in the Lower House. It is also interesting to show again the value of the two-chamber system. Of course, we often say that the First Chamber is becoming more and more political. I think that is also true in part, but fortunately the Upper House is not yet a derivative or copy of the Lower House. Not at all, I'd say. I don't think we should want that either. Then the term of office of the First Chamber. The often cited Curtius was responsible as minister for the introduction of the stepped election of the First Chamber, at that time already, in 1848. It was also considered appropriate at the time that a Chamber of reflection, a Chamber of review, was not a derivative of the political, dominant representation in the Lower House. It was then found that this extended term of office was also appropriate. That was nine years ago. In 1922 it was shortened to six years. It is true that the present method was adopted in 1983. It's just a direct link to the election of the Provincial States, whether you like it or not. Due to the dependence in the sense of political support of the First Chamber and the increasing weight of the First Chamber, you can see that this has made the Provincial State elections a kind of vehicle for the First Chamber elections. This has made things much more political. The cabinet's proposal to extend the term of office of members of the First Chamber and thus to achieve the decoupling of the Provincial State elections is therefore also part of this proposal, because it does justice to both bodies. In fact, the State Committee did not recommend returning to the system before 1983. They have noted, however, that the differences in the political composition of the Chambers are increasing and that support for government coalitions in the Upper Chamber is decreasing. The committee does not expect that a return to 1983 would really matter. The proposal that we have made as a cabinet is therefore not motivated by the desire to act against the development that the State Commission signals. With the current electoral method, we see that the Provincial State elections are becoming more and more the character of First Chamber elections, because they are one-on-one linked in terms of results. And yes, the consequence is that the First Chamber sometimes has a more recent electoral mandate than the Second Chamber. The difference in constitutional status between the Second and First Chambers is largely determined by the method of election. The developments I have just outlined lead to an increase in the political character of the First Chamber in the imagery. Think also of all the top-level debates that take place at First Chamber elections with Provincial State elections. There is less attention to provincial themes. It is more about themes that also play a role in the Lower House elections. In the end, when it comes to the First Chamber elections, you must go to a system in which that self-employed position also comes fully into its own. The question is whether this happens thanks to the current way of choosing. That is why we have made this proposal.
