That is the first thread of Remkes in which the voice of not all citizens is heard. In the letter of 17 October, we explained that we intend to amend the Municipal Law in such a way that the city council can lay down rules on how residents and local associations can submit an alternative proposal for the implementation of collective provisions. This can be with associations or in a direct living environment. This proposal forms part of the modernisation of Article 150 of the Community law. It broadens the participation regulation to include a participation regulation. It went into consultation in December and will be submitted to the House of Representatives by mid 2020. By the way, we work well together with municipalities on this issue. Mr Verkerk asked whether I share the view that social democracy is the basis of our democracy, how I see the role of civil society and whether I can promise that the full cabinet response that is to come will really work on this broad basis of democracy. Civil society is something I very much support, not only in words but also in deeds. I think that is really the strength of our society and, unfortunately, you can see great differences where it is and where it is lacking. You can see the effects right away. You yourself stressed this in your speech. They also asked where the priorities lie. I have heard from several Members that they are otherwise appreciated and appreciated. The categories A and B are the categories we are working with and not category C, which does not mean that it is not important, but there is no support or we think that A and B are more important. That doesn't change the point you're making, because I think this is something that's going on everywhere and you should be able to do it anywhere. I think that idea of right to challenge fits into that, because you are based on the power of society and you do not believe that the government has all the wisdom and can best manage things. So I think that is being properly implemented. Mr Dittrich asked whether we could make voting for Dutch citizens abroad easier and even make it possible to vote for voters on the Internet in the long term. We also believe that it is easy for Dutch people abroad to vote for the House of Representatives and the European Parliament. Several measures have already been taken to make this as easy as possible. For example, the Election Act was amended in 2017, so that the voter no longer has to register for each election. Registering once is enough. For voters abroad there is another ballot that they can get by mail, so that the ballot can arrive in time to vote, because often things went wrong. An amendment to the Tickets Act is under way. The Council of State's opinion was received last Friday. It provides, among other things, that voters in the country can receive a replacement letter vote by e-mail if something goes wrong. If the voter has lost his vote certificate or has not arrived, a new one can be sent by e-mail. That's how digitalization works again. Moreover, we continue to look at possibilities for improvement in the digital field. I dare not say whether we will ever be able to take the step towards voting on the Internet. I would almost say that we could, but we had to deal with those voting machines in 2007. Then we thought everything could be digital, but there were serious doubts as to whether that was sufficiently safe. When it comes to voting, there must be no doubt about the reliability of the result. So I'm not expecting that to happen in the very short term. I hope so, because I also think that this may be possible in a society that is increasingly digitalising. Then there is a question from Mr Dittrich.

Finally, Mr Van Hattem's question as to why the cabinet is not taking measures to prevent municipalities from setting up less polling stations. The number of voting rooms varies between 9,000 and 9,500 per election. In the municipal elections and the consultative referendum on the Wiv in March 2018, there were 9,596 voting rooms. In the European Parliament elections, there were 9.278. It is up to the College of B and W to determine the number of voting rooms. That college is also quite capable of determining, depending on the turnout and the type of election, how many voting rooms are needed in which places, taking into account the distribution across the different villages and neighbourhoods. I have no indications that there are too few voting rooms. I see no reason to take action on this point either.

Mr Schalk asked what the main proposals contained in the Bill on Financing are political parties. An important element is the ban on donations from outside the European Union and the European Economic Area, with the exception of voters residing there, and a gradual adjustment of the level of the subsidy after the Second Parliament elections. The aim is to give political parties more time to adapt their organisation to the new level of funding. At present, this level is adjusted on the first day of the fourth month after the elections.
